---
title: "Equity: who the changes affect"
subtitle: "The case for a different solution for immersion families"
---


::: {.cell}

:::


::: {.callout-important appearance="simple"}
## The argument in brief

- **The plan relies on moving immersion programs.** Scenario A would move 1,586 students in dual language immersion (DLI) programs out of their schools. They are 29% of the students who would have to change schools because of a closure, a program move or the César Chávez change ([§1](#sec-relies)).
- **The programs PPS would move have plenty of families.** Atkinson gets 121 kindergarten applications a year for about 24 seats. Beach gets 96 for 23 ([§2](#sec-demand)).
- **The burdens fall on students of color, in different ways.** In Scenario A, students in the moving programs are 45% Latino and 13% Asian, against 19% and 5% of PPS elementary and middle school students. Black students are only 2% of immersion students. The plan reaches them mainly in two ways: closures, and moving César Chávez's neighborhood program out so the school can be immersion-only. That program is 34% Black. Schools that lose an immersion program are 51% historically underserved and 44% low-income; the schools closing outright are 61% white ([§3](#sec-who)).
- **Immersion families would travel farther.** Their schools would move farther (1.7 vs 1.2 miles). The share of school-age Spanish speakers who live within a mile of a K-5 Spanish site would fall from 52% to 36% ([§4](#sec-burden)).
- **Moving a program loses the families who would have joined it.** In 2023, PPS moved Bridger's Spanish program to Lent. Lent now enrolls 31% fewer immersion kindergartners a year than the two schools averaged per grade before. Measured the same way, the district fell 14%. The 2023 plan had projected 527 students at Lent; it has 256. PPS's own school projections now expect only 80–84% of students in a moved program to follow it. The rest would stay at neighborhood schools. By PPS's own numbers, the moves shrink immersion ([§5](#sec-optout)).
- **The last round of changes also moved neighborhood enrollment.** After the 2023 Southeast changes, fewer Southeast elementary children attend their own neighborhood school. The share fell from 71% to 66%, while it rose elsewhere in PPS. Public data can't separate the part of that drop that was built into the changes from the part that was families' choice ([§6](#sec-capture)).
- **PPS's equity check can't see any of this.** It reports one move rate for each student group. Even so, multilingual learners are already the group PPS moves most often ([§7](#sec-pps)).
- **Southeast Spanish immersion is too small for its demand.** Richmond alone enrolls 89 Japanese kindergartners a year. That is more than Southeast's two Spanish programs combined (70). Atkinson puts about two applicants on its waitlist for every one it offers a seat. Lent's problem is not a shortage of families who want Spanish. Its problem is that it no longer serves its own neighborhood, and the plan would do the same to Rigler ([§8](#sec-southeast)).
:::

## 1. The plan relies on moving immersion programs {#sec-relies}

PPS's own documents put immersion near the center of the plan. Its scenario memo names "underenrolled dual language immersion classrooms" among the problems the modeling responds to. It calls immersion consolidation a "primary driver" of the changes in the Roosevelt/Jefferson region (PPS, *Rightsizing Update: Scenario Release* board memo, October 5, 2026, pp. 1–2 ([BoardBook](https://meetings.boardbook.org/Documents/DownloadPDF/14509719?org=915))). Consolidation means combining programs at fewer schools. Both scenarios would cut elementary Spanish immersion sites from nine to four and move two Mandarin programs. Scenario A would also move Vietnamese immersion.

In Scenario A, 1,586 students are in immersion programs that would move. Another 3,763 attend schools that would close. And 143 students in César Chávez's neighborhood program would move out, so that César Chávez can become an immersion-only school ([§3](#sec-black)). We call all three groups "directly displaced": they would have to change schools because a school closes or because PPS moves their program. Immersion students are **29% of everyone directly displaced**. Yet they are only 13% of PPS enrollment. Boundary changes move many more students, but none of these groups counts them. Public data can't show who those students are.

## 2. "Underenrolled" describes seats, not demand {#sec-demand}

:::
:::


Atkinson and Beach, the two programs with the most demand, have far more families than seats. They are the two Spanish programs with the most applicants in the district. Each gets four to five applications for every kindergarten seat, and dozens of families are waitlisted every year. Their classrooms are small because PPS offers few seats, not because families aren't asking for them.

Other moves are different. James John and Sitton draw little more than one application per seat. Clark has had no waitlist, and its applicants are often placed at programs they ranked higher. The case for consolidating those programs is stronger. A plan that treated these programs differently would take less from immersion families than one that moves them all. The [Demand & scorecard](scorecard.qmd) page has more on demand. It also explains why Lent's own lottery numbers understate its draw.

## 3. Who would have to move, by race and ethnicity {#sec-who}

The plan's burdens fall differently on each racial and ethnic group. The moving immersion programs are mostly Latino, with many Asian students in the Mandarin and Vietnamese programs. Few Black students are in immersion, so the plan reaches Black students mainly through closures and the César Chávez change.

We can see this because PPS's *Enrollment Summary by Race and Ethnicity* (October 2025) gives each immersion program its own row. The table reports each group separately. PPS's combined "historically underserved" share counts Latino, Black, Native American, Pacific Islander and multiracial students together, and it would hide these differences.

Share of each racial and ethnic group among students who would have to change schools in Scenario A, compared with all PPS elementary and middle school students (hollow gray ring). October 2025 enrollment. An open marker means fewer than 5 students, drawn near zero. Hover for counts; the table below has every number and Scenario B.

### Latino students would carry most of the program moves {#sec-latino}

In Scenario A, **45% of students in the moving programs are Latino** (51% in Scenario B). Latino students are 15% of students at the closing schools and 19% of PPS elementary and middle school students. The Spanish programs that would move are 32% to 79% Latino. Of all Latino students who would have to change schools in Scenario A, 54% are in a moving immersion program.

### Black students are rarely in immersion, and the plan reaches them in other ways {#sec-black}

In Scenario A, about 351 Black students would have to change schools. Few of them are in immersion. Most attend a school that would close or César Chávez's neighborhood program.

PPS's own history explains why burdens on Black students need a close look. PPS describes Oregon's 1844 Black exclusion law and the "whites only" clause in the state's 1857 constitution. It says the district "had already adopted racist practices that disadvantage students of color – especially our Black and Native students." In lower Albina, in North and Northeast Portland, urban renewal and Interstate 5 "forcibly displaced thousands of primarily Black residents and businesses." PPS adds that "students bore the cost. Forced busing sent many Black students to schools outside their neighborhood" (PPS, Center for Black Student Excellence, *Historical Context* ([page](https://www.pps.net/fs/pages/20941)), retrieved October 11, 2026). The City of Portland describes how "red lines drawn on a city map would dictate where people of color could live" (City of Portland, Portland Housing Bureau, *N/NE Neighborhood Housing Strategy: Historical Overview* ([page](https://www.portland.gov/phb/nnehousing/historical-overview)), retrieved October 11, 2026).

In 2011 the school board adopted a Racial Educational Equity Policy. In it, PPS "committed to significantly changing its practice in order to achieve and maintain racial equity in education" (PPS, *Racial Educational Equity Policy* (2.10.010-P, approved June 13, 2011; [page](https://www.pps.net/departments/racial-equity-social-justice/racial-educational-equity-policy)), retrieved October 11, 2026).

**Black students are largely missing from immersion.** They are 2% of students in PPS's K-8 immersion programs (91 of 4,457), compared with 8% of elementary and middle school students. Nearly half of them, 43, are in one program: MLK Jr.'s Mandarin program. Both scenarios would move it to Boise-Eliot/Humboldt. That school is 41% Black today.

The Spanish programs that would move enroll almost no Black students: each has fewer than 5. The neighborhood programs in the same buildings are more often Black: Beach 23%, Ockley Green 20%, Sitton 19%, Scott 18%, James John 11%, Beaumont 4%, Atkinson <3%. So in Scenario A, the program moves would move 56 Black students, 43 of them from MLK Jr.

Each moving immersion program (blue circle) compared with the neighborhood program in the same building (hollow gray ring): share of Black students (left) and Latino students (right), October 2025. César Chávez compares its whole K-8 Spanish program with its neighborhood program, which would also move. An open blue circle means fewer than 5 students, drawn near zero.
:::
:::


::: {.source}
**Sources:** PPS, *Enrollment Summary by Race and Ethnicity, October 2025* (separate rows for each school's immersion and neighborhood programs; [PDF](https://resources.finalsite.net/images/v1761606954/ppsnet/p020rdtb2usyi0zikguo/EnrollmentbyRace25-26.pdf)), retrieved October 11, 2026; PPS board packet for October 6, 2026: scenario maps and *Rightsizing Update: Scenario Release* memo, as digitized and transcribed in [pps-explorer](https://github.com/browniefed/pps-explorer) by Jason Brown. Method details: [Sources & methods](methods.qmd).
:::

The plan reaches more Black students in two other ways:

- **Closures.** The schools that would close enroll 247 Black students outside immersion in Scenario A (226 in Scenario B).
- **César Chávez becomes immersion-only.** To make César Chávez a K-5 school with only Spanish immersion, PPS would move its neighborhood program out. Its K-5 students would go to Rosa Parks and its grade 6-8 students to George (PPS, *How Could Your School Be Impacted?*, César Chávez guide (October 8, 2026), page 3, "What is being proposed?" ([page](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/how-could-your-school-be-impacted))). That program has 143 students. **34% are Black**, 4.2 times the share in PPS elementary and middle schools. 80% are historically underserved. This repeats the pattern of Lent's 2023 conversion. The district's equity audit found that the students displaced then "were disproportionately Black and Asian Lent neighborhood families" (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 29). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing initiative: School-by-school analysis* ([PPS SEGC documents](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/segc-documents)), p. 47; see [Research](research.qmd#sec-lent)). We count them in §1 as directly displaced.

Of the 351 Black students who would have to change schools in Scenario A, 70% would move because their school closes.


::: {.cell}
::: {#fig-black-displaced .cell-output-display}

### Asian, Native American and Pacific Islander students {#sec-other-groups}

Asian students are 13% of students in Scenario A's moving programs, compared with 5% of elementary and middle school students. Rose City Park's Vietnamese program and Clark's Mandarin program are mostly Asian. PPS's definition of historically underserved students leaves out Asian students. So PPS's own equity measures, and the "historically underserved" column above, don't show this burden on Vietnamese and Chinese families. Scenario B keeps the Vietnamese program in place (6% Asian).

Native American and Pacific Islander students are rarely in immersion, at least as PPS counts them. PPS's K-8 immersion programs enroll fewer than 5 Native American students and fewer than 5 Pacific Islander students. (To protect privacy, we don't show counts under 5.) These counts include only students who list one race and are not Latino. PPS counts Native American and Pacific Islander students who are also Latino as Latino. It counts students who list more than one race as multiracial (PPS, *Enrollment Summary by Race and Ethnicity, October 2025* (separate rows for each school's immersion and neighborhood programs; [PDF](https://resources.finalsite.net/images/v1761606954/ppsnet/p020rdtb2usyi0zikguo/EnrollmentbyRace25-26.pdf)), retrieved October 11, 2026, page 6 footnote). So the true number of Native students in immersion is likely higher.

The schools that would close enroll 30 Native American and 27 Pacific Islander students outside immersion in Scenario A. These numbers are too small for program-by-program comparisons. Even so, they suggest that immersion reaches few students in either group.

### Some schools that lose a program would become whiter {#sec-left-behind}

Immersion programs bring many of the Latino and Asian students to the schools that host them. At Atkinson, the Spanish program has 124 of the school's 139 Latino students. The neighborhood program that stays is 76% white. At Beaumont Middle School, the Spanish program has 74 of 99 Latino students. Its neighborhood program is 70% white.

PPS's own school guides show the result. They project that **Atkinson's share of students from underserved racial groups would fall from 44% to 21%**, and Beaumont's from 33% to 17%. Scott, MLK Jr. and César Chávez would also fall. Clark and Ockley Green would rise. The guides' projections include boundary changes too, so not all of each change comes from the program move. The measure also leaves out Asian students: Vestal's share falls because the Vietnamese program it would receive is mostly Asian.

The [School changes](schools.qmd) page follows every school the plan touches, including the schools that take in students. It also covers Title I and SUN schools, and whether the moves would separate students by race.

PPS's projected share of students from underserved racial groups at each school that would lose or receive an immersion program: if nothing changes (hollow gray ring) and in Scenario A (filled marker). Both are PPS projections from its school guides. The projections include boundary changes as well as program moves.
:::
:::


::: {.source}
**Sources:** PPS, *How Could Your School Be Impacted?* school guides (per-school PDFs dated October 8, 2026), page 4, "Who would the school serve?", column "Underserved racial groups", with no changes and under each option ([page](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/how-could-your-school-be-impacted)); PPS board packet for October 6, 2026: scenario maps and *Rightsizing Update: Scenario Release* memo, as digitized and transcribed in [pps-explorer](https://github.com/browniefed/pps-explorer) by Jason Brown. Method details: [Sources & methods](methods.qmd).
:::

### Whole schools: income and home language {#sec-whole-school}

PPS publishes income (direct certification) only for whole schools. The table below shows both race and income for the schools involved.

Each school is counted once: Beach, James John and Rose City Park, which close and lose an immersion program, are counted with schools losing an immersion program. Clark is not printed in PPS's report and is left out. These are whole-school figures; for race by program, see the first table in this section.</td>

:::
:::


The schools whose immersion programs would move are **51% historically underserved and 44% low-income**. "Historically underserved" is PPS's term for students of several races and ethnicities, listed in the note under the table. "Low-income" means the state has identified the student as eligible for free meals. The district averages are 35% and 31%. The schools that would close outright are 61% white, 29% underserved and 28% low-income. Scenario B shows the same pattern (56% vs 31% underserved).

These race and income figures describe whole schools, including students in each school's neighborhood program, who don't move. PPS doesn't publish income by program. We can also describe the moving students by home language and English-learner status:


::: {.cell}
::: {#fig-who .cell-output-display}

Share of students whose home language is Spanish, or any language other than English: students in immersion programs that move, students at schools that close (excluding their immersion programs), and all PPS students. October 2025 enrollment.
:::
:::


::: {.source}
**Sources:** PPS, *Enrollment by Language and School*, October 2024 and October 2025 ([PPS Data & Reporting](https://www.pps.net/departments/dataaccountability/data-and-accountability/data-strategy-and-insights/data-and-reporting)); PPS, *Enrollment by Grade and Program Type*, October 2024 and October 2025 ([PPS Data & Reporting](https://www.pps.net/departments/dataaccountability/data-and-accountability/data-strategy-and-insights/data-and-reporting)); PPS board packet for October 6, 2026: scenario maps and *Rightsizing Update: Scenario Release* memo, as digitized and transcribed in [pps-explorer](https://github.com/browniefed/pps-explorer) by Jason Brown; school closures and receiving schools: PPS scenario maps and memo (October 2026) as transcribed in [pps-explorer](https://github.com/browniefed/pps-explorer). Method details: [Sources & methods](methods.qmd).
:::

In Scenario A, **29% of students in the moving programs speak Spanish at home**, and 40% speak a language other than English. At the closing schools, the figures are 3% and 7%. 36% of students in the moving programs are multilingual learners still receiving English language services. Of the Spanish-speaking students directly displaced in Scenario A, **82% are immersion students**.

Two-way immersion depends on these students. It needs native speakers of the program language in every classroom, alongside English speakers.

## 4. Immersion programs move farther from families {#sec-burden}

**How far the school moves (measured).** Immersion programs would move farther than closing schools' students. Students at closing schools mostly move to the next school over, 1.2 miles away on average. Immersion programs would move 1.7 miles on average. Atkinson, Beach, James John and Sitton would each move 1.6–2.5 miles. Scott → Rigler, at 0.7 miles, is the exception.


::: {#tbl-school-miles .cell}
::: {.cell-output-display}

:::
:::


**Access (estimate).** Today, 52% of school-age Spanish speakers live within a mile of a K-5 Spanish immersion site. Under either scenario, 36% would. These counts come from a Census survey, which has a margin of error. Allowing for that error, the drop is 11–20 percentage points.

**Distance from home (model estimate).** PPS doesn't publish where students live, so we estimate it with a model. The model places children where the 2020 Census counted them. For immersion programs, it assumes families live in the area nearest their program's current site. On that basis, the trip from home to school grows by about 1.0 mile for immersion families. It grows by 0.5 mile for families at closing schools.

Model estimate, not measured: average home-to-school distance before and after each change in Scenario A, for children living in the closing school's attendance area, or in the area nearest the immersion program's current school (an assumption about where immersion families live). Hover for details.
:::
:::


::: {.source}
**Sources:** PPS board packet for October 6, 2026: scenario maps and *Rightsizing Update: Scenario Release* memo, as digitized and transcribed in [pps-explorer](https://github.com/browniefed/pps-explorer) by Jason Brown; US Census Bureau, 2020 Census redistricting data (P.L. 94-171), tables P1 and P3, census blocks. Method details: [Sources & methods](methods.qmd).
:::

PPS's report of where each program's students live offers a check. 53% of students in the moving programs live in their school's own attendance area. PPS reports the rest only by high-school area. Placing them there gives an increase of 0.8 to 1.1 miles, close to the model. But the model overstates how far families live from school today: 1.8 miles, against 1.2 miles in the check. The [details and sensitivity checks](#sec-distance-detail) are at the end of this page.

## 5. Moving a program loses the families who would have joined it {#sec-optout}

When a program moves farther away, some families choose their neighborhood school instead. PPS staff described this at a board committee meeting in September: "when we move, for example, a program … we did not see students necessarily leave the district, but they may not have moved with the program" (PPS Board of Education Teaching, Learning, and Enrollment Committee, September 8, 2026, at 33:22 ([video](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=S-pk57P6f_o&t=2002s); automated transcript via [Save PDX Schools](https://savepdxschools.org/transcripts/))). PPS's equity audit of the 2023 Southeast changes found that students in moved programs who stayed in PPS generally followed their program (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 10). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing*, pp. 11, 48–49). Because enrolled students mostly followed, the larger loss is likely among families who haven't joined yet. These are kindergarten families deciding whether to choose a program that is now farther away.

The audit is less clear about who left the moved programs. It says leavers "more often identified as white" (p. 11). The audit reports Latino/a/x students as a separate group alongside White, Asian and Black students. So this most likely means non-Latino white students. But the report does not say how it counted students who are both white and Latino. On home language, the audit contradicts itself. It says leavers "more often speak a language other than English at home" (p. 11). It also says "families who left were disproportionately English-speaking" (p. 49).

The clearest test is the last time PPS consolidated Spanish immersion. In 2023-24, **PPS moved Bridger's Spanish program to Lent** (PPS, *October 2025 Enrollment Summary, 10-year detail*, footnote 6 ("Bridger Spanish Immersion moved to Lent")). Before the move, the two schools had about 55 immersion students per grade. Since then, Lent has enrolled 32 and 43 immersion kindergartners. That is **31% fewer**. Measured the same way, district-wide enrollment fell 14%. So the program lost about **one in five families beyond the district trend** ([details](scorecard.qmd#a-precedent-bridgers-spanish-program-moved-to-lent-in-2023)). The audit also found that the Southeast projections overestimated enrollment at 12 of 20 schools. Of the 7 schools still well short of target, 3 had a program relocation or grade change (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 10). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing*, p. 6).

The 2023 plan expected Lent to grow, not shrink. It turned Lent into a whole-school immersion school, where every class is an immersion class. A strand, by contrast, is an immersion program that shares a school with a neighborhood program. The audit's school-by-school appendix summarizes PPS's Phase 2 impact analysis. That analysis projected that converting Lent to whole-school Spanish immersion "by adding two strands of Spanish immersion from Bridger" would take it "from 277 students in 2021–22 to an estimated 527 students in 2025–26". It projected building use rising "from 43.8% to 87.5%" (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 29). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing initiative: School-by-school analysis* ([PPS SEGC documents](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/segc-documents)), p. 34).

In October 2025, Lent enrolled 256 students, about half that projection. The appendix doesn't say whether the 527 includes pre-kindergarten. Even counting PPS's pre-kindergarten students, Lent is far short. Of the 265 students at Lent the spring before the conversion, neighborhood and immersion alike, 49 (18.5%) had left PPS within two years (p. 45).

Enrollment forecasts by Portland State University (PSU) did not predict the shortfall either. PSU's June 2023 forecast for PPS built in the Bridger → Lent move. Its notes read "Bridger Spanish DLI (grades K-5) moved to Lent ES". The forecast kept almost all of the combined program's 319 students: 314 for Lent in 2023-24. Lent enrolled 273. For three years running, the forecast for Lent was 14–16% too high, while its district-wide forecast was within 1%.

:::
:::


This time, PPS's projections expect some families not to follow. PPS's school guides for the scenarios project each school's enrollment with no changes and under the options (PPS, *How Could Your School Be Impacted?* school guides ("PPS Family-Friendly School Guide", per-school PDFs dated October 8, 2026), page 4, projected enrollment 2027-28 to 2031-32 ([page](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/how-could-your-school-be-impacted))). Their "no changes" figures match PSU's 2026-27 forecast exactly. So we can compare a moved program's arrival with PSU's forecast for that program. In the three moves where nothing else changes at the receiving school, PPS projects that 80–84% of the program's students arrive. Where the guides show the sending school, the rest stay there as neighborhood students:


::: {#tbl-pps-follow .cell}
::: {.cell-output-display}

```

:::
:::


That is close to the Bridger → Lent precedent. It is far from the roughly 98% that PSU assumed for that move in 2023. It also means the loss is part of the plan: PPS expects the consolidated programs to be smaller than the programs they replace.

    </tr>
  </tfoot>
</table>
</div>
```

:::
:::


If Scenario A loses families at the Bridger → Lent rate, about **46 kindergartners a year** who would have joined these programs would go elsewhere. At 7%, it is 16. A public commenter attributed that figure to PPS (see the table note). Over six years of kindergarten classes, that adds up to roughly 279 children. We would expect most to enroll at their neighborhood school instead.

PPS's guides place these students at neighborhood schools. They flag whether each school meets its size and space goals, but they give no capacity figures. PPS publishes each building's current capacity separately, in its School Profiles dashboard ([§8](#sec-southeast)). Either way, the students lost would come from programs at schools that are about half historically underserved ([§3](#sec-who)).

### What staff and families at Lent and Bridger described {#what-staff-and-families-at-lent-and-bridger-described}

The audit's interviews and surveys describe what the 2023 move was like for the schools involved. The appendix identifies speakers only as participants or staff at each school.

- **Families who pushed for the move didn't all stay.** A Lent participant: "A lot of the parents who pushed from Bridger for their kids to come to the school didn't end up staying…they felt like it was out of their way [and that] their students didn't feel supported" (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 29). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing initiative: School-by-school analysis* ([PPS SEGC documents](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/segc-documents)), p. 39).
- **Promises made to families weren't kept.** A Lent participant: "the fourth grade parents were really upset… they were promised it would be a three strand school…and the parents [started] bringing the letters that they were promised" (p. 41). In the main report, families described transition staffing or funding that was "reduced, delayed, or pulled back because of budget constraints" (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 10). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing*, p. 83).
- **The transition was hard on staff and the neighborhood.** "Those years were really terrible, those first few years…There were a lot of tears during the transition" (appendix, p. 36). After 2022-23, 63% of Lent staff left the school, against a 32% average across the affected schools (p. 39). Staff described hostility from neighborhood families who lost access: "We have been cussed out. We have been yelled at…by parents who moved into the neighborhood that do not want their students to speak Spanish, and that feel that it's unfair that their students can't go to their neighborhood school" (p. 41). They also described losing a school children could walk to: "We had a walking school bus every day…We don't have that anymore, because our entire population gets on a bus to go home at the end of the day" (p. 43).
- **Families who moved with the program paid in travel.** "Families who moved from Bridger to Lent described losing walkable access to school, longer commutes, early bus times, transportation uncertainty, and a loss of neighborhood connection" (main report, p. 83).
- **Bridger changed too.** Participants "experienced the Bridger student body as becoming more white and more affluent, while also losing the additional staffing and Title I support that had previously accompanied a higher-poverty enrollment" (appendix, p. 17).
- **Marysville participants questioned whom the consolidation served.** They "noted that the DLI strand was concentrated in a school farther from many Latino/a/x families, while Atkinson, located in a whiter and more affluent neighborhood, retained a single-strand DLI program". They also observed "that some immigrant families may have been hesitant to enroll their children in full Spanish immersion in the broader political climate" (p. 61).

Parents summed up what the move needed: "Whole school DLI is great. It needs to be funded, and it needs to be prioritized" (main report, p. 84).

## 6. Fewer Southeast children now attend their neighborhood school {#sec-capture}

The scenario projections place most students at the schools that new boundaries and program sites assign them to. PPS tracks how often that happens with a measure called the capture rate. The capture rate is the share of children living in a school's attendance area (the neighborhood it serves) who attend that school. District-wide, it has held near 68% for years. So about a third of PPS students already attend a school other than their neighborhood school.


::: {.cell}
::: {#fig-capture .cell-output-display}

Neighborhood capture rate: share of K-12 students living in each elementary attendance area who attend that area's school, October counts. Southeast = the 16 elementary areas around the 2023 Southeast changes, excluding Lent. Hover for counts.
:::
:::


::: {.source}
**Sources:** PPS, *Neighborhood Capture Rate Metrics* (Enrollment Summary by K-12 Students' Neighborhood and Type of School Attended), October 2019 to October 2025 ([current](https://www.pps.net/departments/dataaccountability/data-and-accountability/data-strategy-and-insights/data-and-reporting), [archive](https://www.pps.net/departments/dataaccountability/data-and-accountability/data-strategy-and-insights/archives)). Method details: [Sources & methods](methods.qmd).
:::

After the 2023 Southeast changes, capture in the Southeast elementary areas fell from 71% to 65%. It has stayed there (66% in 2025-26). Over the same years, capture in the rest of PPS rose from 72% to 73%. In the Southeast, the share attending PPS alternative and focus schools rose from 19% to 22%.

Some of this drop was built into the changes and doesn't reflect families' choices. For example, children allowed to finish at their old school after a boundary moved count as attending "another neighborhood school". Also, Creative Science, a focus school, moved into the Bridger building. That put an alternative school inside the Southeast. PPS's reports don't break the drop down, so we can't measure the share due to families' choices. If the drop were mostly students finishing at their old schools, it should fade as they move up. Three years on, Southeast capture has recovered by only 0.6 percentage points.

The Lent area shows what a whole-school immersion site does to its neighborhood. Capture there fell from 63% to 30% when Lent converted. That drop is mostly by design, not a sign of families leaving. PPS assigned Lent's non-immersion neighborhood children to Marysville, and its report counts them as attending "another neighborhood school". Today, 80 of Lent's 256 students live in its area, so most of its families travel from elsewhere. In both scenarios, three of the four K-5 Spanish sites would be whole-school immersion sites like this: Lent, Rigler and César Chávez.

## 7. PPS's equity check misses these patterns {#sec-pps}

PPS's equity check is one slide. It shows the share of kindergarten–8th grade students in each group who would change schools.

:::
:::


Even this table shows **multilingual learners as the most-moved group** (31% in A, 28% in B, against 27% and 24% overall). But it still understates the imbalance in three ways:

- It averages each group over all students in kindergarten through 8th grade, and boundary changes move most of them. That hides what this page shows. Closures fall mostly on whiter, higher-income schools. Program moves fall on schools that are about half underserved, and on students far more likely to speak Spanish at home.
- It counts a move to the next school over the same as a program moved across town.
- It has no row for immersion students, Latino students or home language. It does not measure distance. It does not account for families who stop following a program.

## 8. Southeast Spanish immersion is too small for its demand {#sec-southeast}

In Southeast Portland, PPS offers more kindergarten seats in Japanese than in Spanish. Richmond, a whole-school Japanese immersion school, enrolled 89 kindergartners in October 2025 (512 students in all). Southeast's two Spanish programs, Lent and Atkinson, enrolled 70 between them. Le Monde, a French immersion charter school, enrolled 48. That is more than any Spanish program in the district except Rigler. District-wide, Spanish still has the most immersion seats. The gap is in the Southeast, home to the district's most oversubscribed Spanish program.

The only other Spanish immersion option nearby is a private school. The International School of Portland is in South Waterfront, across the river from inner Southeast. It runs Spanish, Chinese and Japanese immersion tracks and charges tuition. It enrolled 376 students from preschool through 5th grade in 2023-24, including 72 kindergartners. It doesn't publish enrollment by language (National Center for Education Statistics, *Private School Universe Survey*, 2023-24 ([International School of Portland](https://nces.ed.gov/surveys/pss/privateschoolsearch/school_list.asp?Search=1&SchoolName=International+School+of+Portland&State=41)); grade counts as shown by [US News](https://www.usnews.com/education/k12/oregon/international-school-of-portland-324304); languages from the [school's website](https://www.intlschool.org/about-us/about), accessed October 8, 2026).


::: {#tbl-se-seats .cell}

:::
:::


Richmond admits most of the families who apply. Atkinson admits about one in five. Atkinson's waitlist alone, about 49 applications a year, is larger than its kindergarten class. Families can apply to several programs, so this doesn't count children exactly. Still, it suggests that the waitlist alone could likely fill a second Atkinson class each year. PPS's plan moves this program to Lent instead. Its school guide gives the reason: "Moving Atkinson DLI to Lent brings Lent's student numbers above steady target levels (400+ students)" (PPS, *How Could Your School Be Impacted?* school guides ("PPS Family-Friendly School Guide", per-school PDFs dated October 8, 2026), page 4, projected enrollment 2027-28 to 2031-32 ([page](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/how-could-your-school-be-impacted))).

**Lent's problem is its neighborhood, not its distance from the center.** Lent sits about 0.3 miles from the district's edge. Families describe students crossing "205, 92nd and 82nd Ave" to reach Marysville, the new home of Lent's old neighborhood program (Green, T. L., & Hanson, H. (2026, June 29). *Equity audit evaluation of Portland Public Schools' Southeast Enrollment and Program Balancing initiative: School-by-school analysis* ([PPS SEGC documents](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/segc-documents)), p. 46). But Rigler is also about 0.3 miles from the edge. It works as a whole-school Spanish immersion school that serves its own neighborhood: 215 of its 289 students live in its attendance area. At Lent, after PPS moved its neighborhood program out, 80 of 256 do.

Lent's surroundings have plenty of Spanish speakers too. About 393 school-age Spanish speakers live within 1.5 miles of Lent, against 311 near Atkinson. What Lent lacks is the English-speaking families who fill Atkinson's waitlist. They live closer to Atkinson.

:::
:::


**The plan would make Rigler more like Lent.** Rigler's guide (p. 3) says it "would remain open as a focused regional Spanish DLI K-5 school". It also says that "Rigler's non-DLI neighborhood boundary would shift to Scott K-5" (PPS, *How Could Your School Be Impacted?* school guides ("PPS Family-Friendly School Guide", per-school PDFs dated October 8, 2026), page 4, projected enrollment 2027-28 to 2031-32 ([page](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/how-could-your-school-be-impacted))). On the scenario maps, Rigler has its own attendance area today and none in either scenario (PPS board packet for October 6, 2026: scenario maps and *Rightsizing Update: Scenario Release* memo, as digitized and transcribed in [pps-explorer](https://github.com/browniefed/pps-explorer) by Jason Brown). Lent went through the same change in 2023. It ended up with about half the enrollment PPS projected (527 projected, 256 in 2025).

PPS's projection for Rigler doesn't show that risk. It has Rigler growing from 306 students with no changes to 487 with Scott's program, and still 505 in 2031-32. That is Rigler's current enrollment plus the arriving program. The projection treats Rigler as if its neighborhood families keep choosing it after PPS assigns new kindergartners from its area to Scott. The guide also projects Rigler's share of multilingual-learner students falling from 50% to 42%. It projects its share of students in poverty falling from 54% to 46%. The guides don't say whether families in Rigler's area would keep any priority to enroll there.

The buildings have room. Functional capacity is how many students a building can hold. The 2021 Long-Range Facility Plan defines it as a building's classrooms times PPS's standard class size, minus rooms set aside for other uses. Utilization is the share of that capacity in use. The table shows PPS's current figures from its School Profiles dashboard, including 2025-26 utilization. It shows the older plan's figures for comparison.


::: {#tbl-capacity .cell}
::: {.cell-output-display}

:::
:::


Atkinson uses about 71% of its capacity. That leaves room for roughly 150 more students, about 25 per grade. That would fit a second Spanish class in every grade without moving anyone. It would not fit a full doubling of today's program, which has 178 Spanish immersion students in K-5. (PPS's 2021 plan gave Atkinson more room: 567 seats.) A whole-school Spanish program would not fit unless Atkinson's 188 neighborhood-program students moved out. That is what went wrong at Lent.

Lent is the opposite case. Even after Atkinson's program moves in, PPS projects Lent at 401 students, 50% of its capacity. That is still short of PPS's own space-use goal, and Lent's guide marks the goal as not met.

That points to two other ways to serve Southeast families, both closer to where the demand is:

- **Add a second Spanish class at Atkinson**, filled from its waitlist, and keep its neighborhood program. This fits in the current building, as long as PPS doesn't also send Creston's neighborhood children there.
- **Make Creston's building a Southeast Spanish immersion school.** Creston closes in both scenarios, and PPS already plans to send its neighborhood children to Atkinson (PPS, *How Could Your School Be Impacted?* school guides ("PPS Family-Friendly School Guide", per-school PDFs dated October 8, 2026), page 4, projected enrollment 2027-28 to 2031-32 ([page](https://www.pps.net/about/portland-public-schools-information/rightsize/how-could-your-school-be-impacted))). Moving Atkinson's immersion program into Creston instead of Lent would move it 0.8 miles rather than 2.4. The building has room for about 72 students per grade at PPS's current capacity of 429 (PPS, *School Profiles*, 2025-26 (Power BI dashboard: functional capacity, utilization, enrollment, direct certification, capture rate; [page](https://www.pps.net/departments/dataaccountability/data-and-accountability/data-strategy-and-insights/internal-school-profiles)), retrieved October 9, 2026). That is more than twice Atkinson's Spanish program today. Creston would need work first. It is an unreinforced masonry building that PPS has targeted for seismic retrofit (strengthening against earthquakes), according to the PPS facility data compiled in [meub/pps-data](https://github.com/meub/pps-data).

Either way, Lent could get back a neighborhood program serving its own families, many of them Spanish speakers. One question remains. Two-way immersion needs native speakers. About 213 school-age Spanish speakers live within 1.5 miles of Creston, fewer than near Atkinson or Lent. So a Creston site would need to recruit them.

PPS's case for whole-school sites is that teachers need same-grade partners. A second class per grade at Atkinson would give them one. See [Research](research.qmd#sec-single) for what the evidence says about whole-school and strand programs.

## 9. Other options would take less from immersion families {#sec-alternatives}

The data point to options that would take less from immersion families. PPS should evaluate each of these before it decides:

- **Keep high-demand programs where the families are.** Atkinson and Beach have more applicants than seats every year. Consolidate only where demand is low (James John, Sitton, Clark).
- **In the Southeast, grow Spanish immersion where the demand is.** Add a second class at Atkinson, or open a Southeast immersion school in Creston's building, 0.8 miles away instead of 2.4 to Lent. **Restore Lent's neighborhood program** ([§8](#sec-southeast)).
- **Keep Rigler's neighborhood boundary**, the feature that makes it work today. At least guarantee its neighborhood families a seat.
- **Move the neighborhood program instead of the immersion strand.** The plan already does this at César Chávez. There, non-immersion students would move to Rosa Parks so the school can become an immersion hub.
- **Prefer two-class strands to whole-school conversions** where the goal is teacher collaboration. A two-class strand has two immersion classes in each grade. No study we found shows whole-school programs outperform strands ([Research](research.qmd)).
- **Fill "underenrolled" immersion classrooms from the waitlists** instead of moving them.
- **Phase changes in and protect current families.** Let enrolled students finish where they are. Guarantee transportation to any relocated program.
- **Do the equity analysis PPS hasn't published.** Report every change by immersion enrollment, home language, English-learner status, race and income. Include home-to-school distance before and after. Publish the share of families the projections assume will follow each moved program. Judging by the school guides, it is about 82%. Then track how many actually do. PPS has the student-level data to do this.

## Details: distance estimates and every change {#sec-distance-detail}

### Estimated home-to-school distance {#estimated-home-to-school-distance}

@fig-home in §4 shows the estimate for each change. The model places children by 2020 census block, a small area the Census reports on. For closures, it uses the children living in the closing school's attendance area. Their new school is the one the scenario map assigns. For immersion programs, it uses the children nearest the program's current site for that language. This is an assumption. Their new school is the program's new site.

:::
:::


**Checked against where students live.** PPS's *School Enrollment by Neighborhood of Residence* report counts how many of each program's students live in the school's own attendance area. For the rest, it gives the high-school area they live in. We place those students on census blocks within that area in two ways: spread over all the area's children, or over the half living closest to the program's current school.

### Limits of this analysis {#caveats}

- Lottery figures count applications, not children.
- Race and income are whole-school figures; PPS doesn't publish them by program.
- Home-to-school distances and the number of families who don't follow are estimates.
- This page does not count students moved by boundary changes.
- This is an independent analysis, not a PPS publication.

See [Sources & methods](methods.qmd#equity) for full definitions.
